## 4. Identity, data, and the security posture

7 findings — 3 medium, 4 low. 47 files read.

### How it actually works

Identity is one Cognito pool (`quantum-workspace`, `infra/workspace/cognito.yaml`) with a public SPA client: SRP + refresh only (no `ALLOW_USER_PASSWORD_AUTH`), `PreventUserExistenceErrors: ENABLED`, 60-minute ID/access tokens, Google federated with `email_verified` mapped through so the QPU spend gate isn't a dead end for federated users. A `PostConfirmation` Lambda is inlined in the template and is written to never throw, because a throwing trigger breaks sign-up itself.

Every authenticated backend uses the same shape: an API Gateway HTTP API `CognitoJwt` authorizer (`IdentitySource: $request.header.Authorization`, issuer + `audience: UserPoolClientId`), and handlers that read identity *only* from `event.requestContext.authorizer.jwt.claims.sub`. `lambda/sync/index.mjs` is a versioned KV — GET/PUT/DELETE on one `quantum-workspace-progress` row keyed by `sub`, optimistic concurrency via a `ConditionExpression` on `version`, a 262,144-byte UTF-8 cap, and a `ValidationException`→413 backstop. `web/src/lib/sync-client.ts` sends the **ID** token (not the access token), so the audience check passes and `claims.email` is present — which `index.mjs:52` persists onto the row.

`lambda/review-email` then reads that row. `createSenderCore` scans the whole progress table, counts due `qc:card:*` via a verbatim port of the app's `isValidCardState`, refuses to send while SES is in the sandbox (`GetAccount().ProductionAccessEnabled`), requires `remindersOn === true` in a *separate* `quantum-review-email-prefs` table (so sync's full-item PUT can never clobber consent), enforces a 7-day cadence marker, and emits EMF metrics that ring `quantum-review-email-send-failures`. Unsubscribe is an unauthenticated Function URL verifying `b64url(sub).b64url(HMAC(sub))` with `timingSafeEqual`.

Local data is namespaced per owner (`progress-owner.ts`: anon == the flat canonical space, accounts get `qc:o:<sub>:`), translated to canonical only at the wire boundary, so a shared browser can't leak one learner's cards to the next.

Secrets have largely moved right: tutor and stripe now read Secrets Manager by name with ARN-scoped `GetSecretValue`. Two remain plaintext in Lambda env (`UNSUB_SECRET`, `EDGE_SECRET`), and `RATE_CARD` will join them at cutover. Response headers come from `customHttp.yml` — HSTS, nosniff, Referrer-Policy, Permissions-Policy, and a CSP that constrains framing/base/object only.

The prior audit's account-ID cleanup mostly held: a repo-wide 12-digit scan finds the production account only twice. `?next=` (issue #225) is still bypassable — `new URL("/\\evil.com", origin)` resolves to `https://evil.com/`.

### Done well

- infra/github-oidc-drift-role.yaml:45-50 — the OIDC trust policy is exactly right and is the thing this audit was told to suspect: `StringEquals` (not `StringLike`) on BOTH `token.actions.githubusercontent.com:aud` and the fully-qualified `sub` `repo:AltivumInc-Admin/quantum-computing:ref:refs/heads/main`. No wildcard, no `ref:refs/heads/*`, no environment-only match. The parameter description even names the failure mode it avoids.
- lambda/tutor/template.yaml:154-166 and lambda/stripe/template.yaml:159-162 — the prior audit's "two stacks inject secrets as plaintext Lambda environment variables" is FIXED for both money-adjacent stacks: only `SECRET_ID` (a name) is in the environment, and `secretsmanager:GetSecretValue` is scoped to a single secret ARN with the `-??????` suffix rather than `*`, with the comment noting the tutor role must not be able to read quantum-stripe's key.
- lambda/review-email/template.yaml:104-110 — `ses:SendEmail` is granted with a `StringEquals` condition on `ses:FromAddress`, so even a fully compromised sender cannot mail as any other identity verified in the live account. Paired with review-email-core.mjs:214-219, which aborts the entire run when `ProductionAccessEnabled !== true` rather than fanning out hard-failing sends.
- lambda/review-email/review-email-core.mjs:260-267 — consent is genuinely opt-in and fails closed: an absent prefs row or `remindersOn` absent/false skips the learner, the legacy `optOut` is kept as a veto, and prefs live in their own table (template.yaml:53-55) precisely so lambda/sync's full-item PUT can never resurrect a withdrawn consent.
- lambda/sync/template.yaml:75-77 — a stage-level `ThrottlingRateLimit: 5 / BurstLimit: 10` sized from the client's actual debounce cadence, layered over `ReservedConcurrentExecutions` and the byte-length cap at index.mjs:84. The comment does the arithmetic on what an unthrottled 256KB PUT flood costs against a PAY_PER_REQUEST table. This is a real abuse-and-cost boundary, not a checkbox.
- infra/workspace/cognito.yaml:233-241 — Google's OIDC `email_verified` claim is mapped onto the pool attribute, with the comment explaining that mapped addresses are unverified by default and that without it the QPU spend gate is unreachable for every Google sign-in. lambda/qpu/qpu-core.mjs:763 then actually consumes it (`claims.email_verified === "true"`).
- lambda/sync/index.test.mjs:130-150 — the DELETE path is tested for exactly the property that matters: the DynamoDB Key is deep-equal to `{ userId: { S: "user-9" } }` (identity from the token, nothing else), and a missing `sub` returns 401 with `ddb.calls.length === 0`, i.e. rejected before any AWS call.
- scripts/check-rate-parity.mjs:12-19 — the deployed-config check is value-blind by construction and the header explains why a hash prefix is NOT an acceptable substitute (a rate factor is a short low-entropy string, dictionary-recoverable). That is the correct reading of rule 6 applied to a CI log.

### Findings

#### `f66ea059e` `failover.sh engage` arms a CodeBuild webhook with no contributor filter on a PUBLIC repo, giving any fork PR code execution under a role that can list the live Cognito user pool

`infra/ci-standby/failover.sh:83-89`

**medium** · Confirmed · security · observed live · **split verdict** · fix plan 05

**Claim.** The webhook created by `engage` filters on EVENT only. AWS's own guidance (and security bulletin 2026-002-AWS) is that PR-triggered CodeBuild webhooks must carry an `ACTOR_ACCOUNT_ID`/`GITHUB_ACTOR_ACCOUNT_ID` filter, because on a public repository any GitHub user can open a fork PR and have their code executed. The inline buildspec runs `npm ci` (arbitrary package.json lifecycle scripts), `pip install -e ".[dev,full]"` and `make test` on the PR head, inside a project whose ServiceRole holds `cognito-idp:ListUsers` on the production `quantum-workspace` pool and `codeconnections:GetConnectionToken`.

> **Correction.** The cited bulletin 2026-002-AWS is real, but it is not quite what the finder says: it covers *insufficiently anchored regexes in ACTOR_ACCOUNT_ID filters that already existed* on AWS-managed repos, not the total absence of one. It supports the finding (no filter is strictly worse than an unanchored one) but the direct authority is the CodeBuild "Best practices for using webhooks" page, which recommends ACTOR_ACCOUNT_ID filters for public repositories by name. Also worth noting in the writeup: the inline buildspec the README leans on is a genuine AWS-recommended control, so the omission is 3-of-4 missing rather than naivety.

**Impact.** While engaged, an anonymous internet user can exfiltrate the entire live learner roster (every registered email address) and the CodeConnections GitHub token from build memory. `engage` is the documented incident procedure, run under time pressure during a GitHub Actions outage. Verified NOT currently armed (`aws codebuild batch-get-projects` returns `webhook: null`), which is the only reason this is not high.

**Evidence.** `--filter-groups '[ [{"type": "EVENT", "pattern": "PULL_REQUEST_CREATED, PULL_REQUEST_UPDATED, PULL_REQUEST_REOPENED"}], [{"type": "EVENT", "pattern": "PUSH"}, {"type": "HEAD_REF", "pattern": "^refs/heads/main$"}] ]'` — the PUSH group is ref-scoped; the PULL_REQUEST group has no second filter at all. The role it runs as: infra/ci-standby/template.yaml:103-106 `Action: cognito-idp:ListUsers` / `Resource: $ARN}:userpool/$USER_POOL_ID`. infra/ci-standby/README.md reasons about PR safety only in terms of the buildspec being inline ("can't be broken by a PR touching a buildspec path") and never mentions forks.

**Verifier (reproduces) — Confirmed, medium, observed live.** Reproduces as described. `infra/ci-standby/failover.sh:83-89` creates the webhook with a PR filter group containing an EVENT filter and nothing else; `grep -niE "PullRequestBuildPolicy|requiresCommentApproval|ACTOR_ACCOUNT_ID|FORK" infra/ci-standby/template.yaml infra/ci-standby/failover.sh` returns no matches, and `aws codebuild batch-get-projects --names quantum-ci-standby --region us-east-2 --query 'projects[0].source'` shows the live project carries no `pullRequestBuildPolicy` (absent = DISABLED = builds trigger automatically), so there is no compensating control. The path is not dead: `gh repo view` confirms visibility PUBLIC, and `aws iam get-role-policy --role-name quantum-ci-standby-codebuild --policy-name founding-ten-verify` returns a live `cognito-idp:ListUsers` on `$USER_POOL_ID` alongside `codeconnections:GetConnectionToken` — the role exists and is armed today; only the webhook is missing (`webhook: null`, which is why this is medium not high). AWS's "Best practices for using webhooks" page lists four controls for public-repo webhooks — ACTOR_ACCOUNT_ID filters, FILE_PATH filters, a scoped-down build role, and an inline/S3 buildspec — and this stack implements only the fourth, which stops a PR from changing *which* commands run but not what `npm ci` (pretest/prebuild hooks), `pip install -e` (PR build backend) and `make test` (`pytest tests/`) execute from the PR head.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Refuted, low, observed live.** The exploit chain does not work. `engage` (failover.sh:83-89) calls `create-webhook` without `--pull-request-build-policy`, and AWS's default for that field is `requiresCommentApproval: ALL_PULL_REQUESTS` with `approverRoles: [GITHUB_ADMIN, GITHUB_MAINTAIN, GITHUB_WRITE]`, "enabled by default for all newly created CodeBuild projects" (docs.aws.amazon.com/codebuild/latest/userguide/pull-request-build-policy.html) — so an untrusted fork PR gets build status "Failed / Pull request approval required for starting a build" and a repo admin/maintainer/writer must post `/codebuild_run(<sha>)` before any PR code executes. `aws codebuild batch-get-projects --names quantum-ci-standby --region us-east-2` shows created 2026-07-16, six months after that feature was already shipping (bulletin 2026-002-AWS, 2026-01-15, cites it as an existing mitigation), so the default applies; the same call confirms `webhook: null` today. What is real but far smaller than claimed: `aws iam get-role-policy --role-name quantum-ci-standby-codebuild --policy-name founding-ten-verify` shows the deployed role does hold `cognito-idp:ListUsers` on the live `quantum-workspace` pool (`aws cognito-idp describe-user-pool --user-pool-id $USER_POOL_ID` → 14 users), against AWS's "scope down the permissions for your build IAM role" guidance — reachable by an already-trusted contributor or a malicious npm dependency, not by an anonymous internet user. Adding an explicit `ACTOR_ACCOUNT_ID` filter is still worthwhile belt-and-braces, since `grep -rniE "fork|ACTOR_ACCOUNT_ID|pullRequestBuildPolicy" infra/ci-standby/` returns nothing — the safety rests entirely on an unstated service default.

*Split verdict: reproduces said Confirmed; correct as written said Refuted. This one needs a human.*

*Raised independently by 1 other reader(s) on a different dimension: `f9805323f` (Build, CI, deploy, and drift).*

#### `f50e54cb6` The CI drift role grants account-wide `lambda:ListFunctions` on a false premise — the API does return `Environment.Variables`, and nothing in the repo calls it

`infra/github-oidc-drift-role.yaml:65-68`

**medium** · Confirmed · security · observed live · **split verdict** · fix plan 05

**Claim.** The statement is justified by a comment asserting ListFunctions "returns metadata only (no code, no environment secrets)." That is factually wrong: `FunctionConfiguration` includes `Environment`, and ListFunctions omits only State/LastUpdateStatus/RuntimeVersionConfig. I verified this against the live account — `aws lambda list-functions --query 'Functions[].{name:FunctionName,envKeys:keys(Environment.Variables)}'` returned env keys including `UNSUB_SECRET` (quantum-review-email-sender, quantum-review-email-unsubscribe) and `EDGE_SECRET` (quantum-qpu-submit). Separately, neither CI script needs it: scripts/check-lambda-drift.mjs:39 uses a hardcoded `FUNCTIONS` list and calls only `get-function`/`get-function-configuration`, and scripts/check-rate-parity.mjs:36-51 calls `get-function-configuration`.

> **Correction.** The impact names the wrong secrets. EDGE_SECRET (quantum-qpu-submit), UNSUB_SECRET (quantum-review-email-*) and, after cutover, RATE_CARD all live on `quantum-*` functions, which this role already reads through the intended resource-scoped `lambda:GetFunctionConfiguration` statement that check-rate-parity.mjs genuinely calls — I confirmed `aws lambda get-function-configuration --function-name quantum-qpu-submit --query 'keys(Environment.Variables)'` returns EDGE_SECRET. Removing ListFunctions does not protect any of them. What ListFunctions uniquely adds is the other 188 environment-carrying functions in this shared account: unrelated production workloads including `tj-scents-api-dev-*`, whose env keys are STRIPE_SECRET_KEY, STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET and DB_PASSWORD. The cross-tenant blast radius is therefore larger than the finder claimed, but it lands on other businesses' credentials rather than on Quantum Learner's. Reachability is also narrower than "any workflow on main" implies from a public repo: the trust policy pins `sub` to `repo:AltivumInc-Admin/quantum-computing:ref:refs/heads/main`, so fork PRs (which run as `refs/pull/N/merge`) cannot assume it — it needs write access to main or a compromised action, and drift.yml pins actions to mutable `@v4` tags.

> **Correction.** The named secrets are not what this grant exposes. `EDGE_SECRET` (quantum-qpu-submit), `UNSUB_SECRET` (quantum-review-email-sender/-unsubscribe) and the future `RATE_CARD` (quantum-tutor, quantum-qpu-submit) all sit on `quantum-*` functions, which the role already reads through the required, correctly-scoped statement — `aws lambda get-function-configuration --function-name quantum-qpu-submit --query 'keys(Environment.Variables)'` returns `EDGE_SECRET`, and GetFunctionConfiguration on `function:quantum-*` is what both CI scripts actually need. Deleting the ListFunctions statement would protect none of the three. Its real marginal exposure is the environment of every NON-quantum function in the shared account, which is a different and arguably worse leak: an unrelated business's live Stripe secret key and database password.

**Impact.** Any workflow on main can dump every Lambda environment in account $AWS_ACCOUNT (shared, not quantum-only) in one call. `EDGE_SECRET` is the shared secret that stops the QPU HTTP API being hit directly to bypass the WAF (lambda/qpu/template.yaml:38-44); `UNSUB_SECRET` forges unsubscribe tokens for any learner. Once `RateCardSecret` is set at cutover, `RATE_CARD` joins them — the one number rule 6 says must exist only in private notes and deployed configuration, readable through a grant the repo added for a check that never uses it.

**Evidence.** ```
\# ListFunctions cannot be resource-scoped; it is read-only and returns
\# metadata only (no code, no environment secrets).
- Effect: Allow
  Action: lambda:ListFunctions
  Resource: "*"
```

**Verifier (reproduces) — Confirmed, medium, observed live.** The comment at infra/github-oidc-drift-role.yaml:64-65 is factually false and the grant is live, not theoretical. `aws lambda list-functions --region us-east-2 --query 'Functions[?Environment.Variables!=`null`].{name:FunctionName,envKeys:keys(Environment.Variables)}'` returned environment maps for 198 of 268 functions — the JMESPath filter is client-side, so ListFunctions plainly ships `Environment.Variables` in the response body. `aws iam get-role-policy --role-name quantum-ci-drift-check --policy-name read-lambda-code-only` shows the deployed inline policy carries `lambda:ListFunctions` on `Resource: "*"`, and `gh variable list --repo AltivumInc-Admin/quantum-computing` shows AWS_DRIFT_ROLE_ARN is set, so drift.yml assumes this role daily. A repo-wide grep for `list-functions|ListFunctions|listFunctions` (excluding node_modules) hits only the template itself — check-lambda-drift.mjs:39 iterates a hardcoded FUNCTIONS array via get-function, and check-rate-parity.mjs:33 does the same via get-function-configuration — so the statement is dead weight and deleting it breaks nothing.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Partly true, medium, observed live.** The comment at infra/github-oidc-drift-role.yaml:64-68 is indeed false: AWS docs state ListFunctions omits only State/StateReasonCode/StateReason/LastUpdateStatus*/RuntimeVersionConfig, and `aws lambda list-functions --region us-east-2` returned `Environment.Variables` for every function in account $AWS_ACCOUNT, including unrelated tenants (tj-scents-api-dev-* carries DB_PASSWORD, STRIPE_SECRET_KEY, STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET). The grant is unused — `grep -rniI "list-functions|listfunctions" . --exclude-dir=node_modules` hits only the template; check-lambda-drift.mjs:75,91 and check-rate-parity.mjs:40 use hardcoded name lists with get-function/get-function-configuration. It is live, not hypothetical: `aws iam get-role-policy --role-name quantum-ci-drift-check --policy-name read-lambda-code-only` returns the `lambda:ListFunctions` on `"*"` statement verbatim, and `gh variable list` shows AWS_DRIFT_ROLE_ARN wired up. Not high, because the trust policy pins `sub` to `repo:AltivumInc-Admin/quantum-computing:ref:refs/heads/main` via StringEquals, so a fork PR cannot assume it — exploiting this needs write access to main. Fix is to delete the statement; nothing calls it.

*Split verdict: reproduces said Confirmed; correct as written said Partly true. This one needs a human.*

*Raised independently by 1 other reader(s) on a different dimension: `fb854583f` (Build, CI, deploy, and drift).*

#### `f6abb88d4` Account deletion leaves the wallet and QPU ledger rows behind, and the UI's list of what will be deleted omits them

`web/src/components/auth/delete-account.tsx:87-129`

**medium** · Partly true · correctness · observed live · fix plan 09

**Claim.** The flow deletes exactly three server-side things: the sync progress row (step 1), the reminder-prefs row (step 2), and the Cognito user (step 3). Nothing deletes the learner's `quantum-stripe-wallet` WALLET# row or their `quantum-qpu-ledger` row. `git grep -n "DeleteItem" -- lambda/stripe lambda/qpu` returns exactly one hit, and it is an assertion that the capability must not exist: lambda/stripe/template.test.mjs:108 `assert.doesNotMatch(b, /dynamodb:DeleteItem/, "the handler never deletes; do not grant it")`.

> **Correction.** The wallet half of the impact is currently vacuous, and the finder's stronger evidence is elsewhere. `aws dynamodb scan --table-name quantum-stripe-wallet --region us-east-2 --select COUNT` returns Count: 0 — there are no wallet rows at all today (the storefront is closed per R15 and the Founding Ten gift has not been issued), so no balance, tier, subscriptionStatus or clawbackOwedCredits survives anything, and "purchased top-ups become permanently unreachable" is hypothetical rather than present. Conversely the finder understated the live half: the rendered privacy page (web/src/components/privacy/privacy-page-content.tsx:34,53) discloses storing "a record of that run (device, shot count, cost, and a hash of the circuit)" and promises "you can delete all of it, permanently, yourself" (web/src/i18n/locales/en.ts:914), while the retention paragraph at en.ts:945 enumerates only the three things the flow actually deletes — that published, deployed promise, not the omitted `<li>` in the confirm dialog, is the load-bearing defect.

> **Correction.** Three parts of the reasoning are wrong. (1) "The only funded wallets are the Founding Ten" is false — quantum-stripe-wallet has ItemCount 0, so there are no wallet rows at all; balance/tier/clawbackOwedCredits surviving deletion has never happened. (2) Rule 10 is misapplied: it governs credit *expiry* ("purchased top-ups never expire"), is recorded as "Not yet implemented", and says nothing about voluntary self-service deletion, where forfeiture is ordinary product behaviour rather than expiry. (3) The Stripe handler's inability to delete is deliberate, not an oversight — template.test.mjs:108 asserts dynamodb:DeleteItem must never be granted, the table carries DeletionPolicy: Retain + PITR, and the row holds clawbackOwedCredits, a debt a user must not be able to erase by deleting their account. The fixable defect is therefore the disclosure (the UI blurb's "all data" and the privacy policy's deletion clause should name the records that are deliberately retained), not the retention itself.

**Impact.** After "delete my account", the learner's balance, tier, subscriptionStatus, `clawbackOwedCredits` and lifetime QPU spend survive indefinitely, keyed to a `sub` that no longer exists, and the disclosure list at lines 175-180 never mentions them. Because identity is the Cognito sub, re-registering mints a new one — so purchased top-ups, which rule 10 promises never expire, become permanently unreachable while the debt row also survives. Metering is off and the only funded wallets are the Founding Ten, which is why this is medium and not high.

**Evidence.** The three server steps are `await deleteProgress();` (line 91), `await deleteReminderPrefs();` (line 104), `await deleteUser();` (line 120). The rendered promise is `{syncConfigured && <li>{t("deleteAccount.itemProgress")}</li>} {prefsConfigured && <li>{t("deleteAccount.itemPrefs")}</li>} <li>{t("deleteAccount.itemAccount")}</li> <li>{t("deleteAccount.itemLocal")}</li>` — four items, no wallet, no ledger.

**Verifier (reproduces) — Partly true, medium, observed live.** The mechanism is real: delete-account.tsx:91/104/120 deletes only the sync row (lambda/sync/index.mjs:127), the prefs row (lambda/review-email/review-email-core.mjs:416) and the Cognito user, and nothing anywhere deletes the QPU ledger's `CRED#<sub>`/`USER#<sub>` rows (lambda/qpu/qpu-core.mjs:258, 304, 403) or the `quantum-qpu-tasks` rows keyed by `userId`. I confirmed this is live, not dormant: `aws amplify get-app --app-id d1ao02to23x85y --query 'app.environmentVariables'` shows `NEXT_PUBLIC_QPU_URL` set (and no `NEXT_PUBLIC_BILLING_URL`), and `aws dynamodb scan --table-name quantum-qpu-ledger --region us-east-2 --projection-expression pk` returns a live `USER#f1bb…`/`CRED#f1bb…` pair plus 5 rows in `quantum-qpu-tasks` carrying `userId` and `circuitHash`. No cleanup path exists to refute it — `describe-time-to-live` on quantum-qpu-ledger shows TTL on `expiresAt`, which qpu-core.mjs:459 writes only onto the DAY# killswitch counter, and `aws cognito-idp describe-user-pool --user-pool-id $USER_POOL_ID` returns `LambdaConfig: {}`, so there is no post-deletion trigger either.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Partly true, low, observed live.** The code reads exactly as claimed: delete-account.tsx:88-129 performs deleteProgress / deleteReminderPrefs / deleteUser and nothing else, and the list at :176-179 names four items. But `aws dynamodb describe-table --table-name quantum-stripe-wallet --region us-east-2 --query 'Table.[TableName,ItemCount,TableSizeBytes]'` returns ItemCount 0 / 0 bytes, so no wallet row has ever existed in production and the entire money half of the impact is hypothetical (the same command on quantum-qpu-ledger returns 2 items, quantum-qpu-tasks 5). What actually stands today is a promise gap, not a data-retention bug: en.ts:427 says deletion "Permanently removes your account and all data" and privacyUi.retentionDelete (en.ts:944-945) frames server-side data as deletable, while the same policy at en.ts:928-929 discloses a stored hardware-run record that nothing in the repo ever deletes. Severity low: the surviving rows are a handful of QPU ledger/task rows on a surface whose lifetime cap is 0, with a closed storefront and metering off by decision.

#### `f54ce715f` lambda/tutor/policy.json still publishes the production AWS account id in a public repo, and is now a dead Bedrock policy the README tells you to attach

`lambda/tutor/policy.json:9`

**low** · Confirmed · security · observed live · **split verdict** · fix plan 08

**Claim.** A repo-wide scan for 12-digit ids (`git grep -nP '(?<![0-9])[0-9]{12}(?![0-9])'`) finds the owner's production account $AWS_ACCOUNT in exactly two tracked files: this one and scripts/founding-credit/cohort-2026-08.json:9 (where it is a deliberate `expectedAccountId` guard). The prior audit's finding #50 — public repo publishes AWS account IDs — was recorded RESOLVED 2026-08-16; this file was not touched (last commit 566c370) and still carries it. Worse, CLAUDE.md records that the tutor moved off Bedrock on 2026-08-17 and that "the old Bedrock IAM grant … [is] gone from the template," so this document grants only `bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream` and no `secretsmanager:GetSecretValue`.

> **Correction.** The finder frames the surviving literal as a missed scrub ("this file was not touched ... and still carries it"). It was not missed — docs/codebase-audit-2026-08-16.md:76-77 records the resolution of prior finding #10 as deliberately keeping two literals, naming this one because "`lambda/tutor/policy.json` is a live IAM document." The real defect is that this exception's sole stated justification expired one day later: after the 2026-08-17 Bedrock migration the file is not a live IAM document. Separately, docs/feature-optimization.md:1027 already recorded a fix retiring policy.json as "DONE 2026-07-20", which was never applied (last commit to the file is 566c370, 2026-06-18) — so this is a twice-closed item, not a new one. Severity stays low: an AWS account id is an identifier, not a credential (the prior audit's verifier established this and I agree), and the broken deploy is a documented fallback, not the shipped SAM path.

> **Correction.** Two things in the reasoning are wrong. (1) policy.json was not overlooked by the 2026-08-16 scrub — docs/codebase-audit-2026-08-16.md:75-77 names it and scripts/founding-credit/cohort-2026-08.json as the two literals "deliberately left in place," policy.json because it "is a live IAM document." The real point is not that it was missed but that the recorded rationale has since expired: after the 2026-08-17 move off Bedrock it is no longer a live IAM document, so the exclusion's basis is gone. (2) Calling it "the one remaining incidental disclosure of the live account id" implies exposure that scrubbing would remove; it would not — the identical id is deliberately committed at scripts/founding-credit/cohort-2026-08.json:9 in the same public repo. Also, the prior finding is #10 in the "Security and accessibility" section (docs/codebase-audit-2026-08-16.md:517), not #50.

**Impact.** Two things at once. (1) The one remaining incidental disclosure of the live account id, in a file that reads as an authoritative live IAM document. (2) lambda/tutor/README.md:103 still instructs `--policy-document file://lambda/tutor/policy.json`; anyone taking that documented fallback today deploys a tutor role with dead Bedrock permissions and no way to read the `quantum-tutor` secret the handler now requires — a deploy that cannot work, produced by following the docs.

**Evidence.** `"$ARN",` — the only statement in the file besides Logs is `"Action": ["bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"]`.

**Verifier (reproduces) — Confirmed, low, observed live.** Both halves reproduce. `git grep -n '$AWS_ACCOUNT'` returns exactly two tracked hits — lambda/tutor/policy.json:9 and scripts/founding-credit/cohort-2026-08.json:9 — and `gh repo view AltivumInc-Admin/quantum-computing --json visibility` returns PUBLIC. The dead-policy half is worse than described: policy.json grants only `bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream` + Logs, while index.mjs:549 reads the API key via `GetSecretValueCommand`; template.yaml:160-165 grants `secretsmanager:GetSecretValue` and contains no `bedrock:` action at all, so the SAM path works and the raw-CLI path README.md:34,103 documents cannot — and README.md:38-40 sits three lines below the policy.json pointer stating the function reads its key from Secrets Manager. I ran `cd lambda/tutor && node --test template.test.mjs`: 18/18 pass, including line 172's "a Bedrock grant survived a migration that removed every Bedrock call" — that assert reads `body("TutorFunction")` from template.yaml only, so policy.json escapes the very guard written to catch it.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Partly true, low, read.** The staleness half is exactly as described: lambda/tutor/policy.json grants only `bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream` plus Logs, while lambda/tutor/index.mjs:549 reads the Anthropic key via `GetSecretValueCommand({SecretId: process.env.SECRET_ID})` and lambda/tutor/template.yaml:154-165 is the only place granting `secretsmanager:GetSecretValue` — so the raw-CLI fallback at README.md:102-109 (put-role-policy from policy.json, then `SECRET_ID=quantum-tutor`) builds a role that cannot start the handler. The disclosure half does not survive: `git grep -n '$PROD_ACCOUNT'` returns exactly two tracked hits, and docs/codebase-audit-2026-08-16.md:75-77 records both by path as **deliberately** retained ("`lambda/tutor/policy.json` is a live IAM document"), so this was an explicit exclusion, not an untouched miss — and the same id is already published on purpose at scripts/founding-credit/cohort-2026-08.json:9, meaning scrubbing policy.json un-publishes nothing. Severity is low: the security impact is nil (the prior audit's own verifier adjudicated account ids as identifiers, not secrets, with no exploitable step), and the doc-drift failure is loud and immediate (AccessDenied on first invoke) on the explicitly-secondary deploy path — SAM is labelled "recommended" at README.md:34.

*Split verdict: reproduces said Confirmed; correct as written said Partly true. This one needs a human.*

*Raised independently by 1 other reader(s) on a different dimension: `fd41306fa` (The tutor after Bedrock).*

#### `f63d74755` A test asserts the CSP must NOT contain script-src, style-src or default-src, so hardening it turns CI red

`web/__tests__/infra/custom-http.test.ts:179-183`

**low** · Partly true · security · ran locally · fix plan 11

**Claim.** customHttp.yml:43 ships `Content-Security-Policy: frame-ancestors 'self'; object-src 'none'; base-uri 'self'` — no directive constrains script execution or data egress, so the header does nothing against XSS or exfiltration. That is the prior audit's finding #49. What is new is that a test now locks the gap in place with negative assertions, the same anti-pattern the prior audit flagged for the sponsorship copy ("the copy-honesty test REQUIRES the false sponsorship string so fixing copy turns CI red").

> **Correction.** The defect is real but mis-analogized. The sponsorship-copy precedent locked a FALSE customer-facing string into shipped product copy — a live lie the test kept alive. This test locks the ABSENCE of an unshipped hardening, which is a documented TODO, not a falsehood; the assertion was added in the same commit as the header it guards (1612ceb, #156). "Must be argued down before it can be shipped" is unsupported: there is no CODEOWNERS or approval barrier, and the fix is a five-line edit in the same PR. The suggested `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline' 'wasm-unsafe-eval' blob:` is also untested against this runtime — Pyodide boots via `importScripts` of a blob URL after a cross-origin `fetch` to cdn.jsdelivr.net (web/public/pyodide.worker.js:50-67), and the bundled JupyterLite/Monaco surfaces at /lab are unverified under any script-src, which is plausibly why the header was staged rather than shipped.

> **Correction.** The finder's proposed replacement, `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline' 'wasm-unsafe-eval' blob:`, does not in fact "satisfy both constraints the comment names" — it would break shipped behavior twice over. web/src/lib/pyodide-runtime.ts:55 keeps https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/pyodide/v0.29.0/full/ as the worker's boot failover; the SRI-pinned bootstrap survives (fetched, then run from a blob URL — web/public/pyodide.worker.js:63), but loadPyodide({indexURL}) then loads pyodide.asm.js via `globalThis.importScripts(url)` in the worker branch of web/public/pyodide/pyodide.js, with `await import(e)` as its only catch-path — both blocked without cdn.jsdelivr.net in script-src, and the '/**' pattern applies the CSP to the worker's own response. Separately, `grep -rl 'new Function(' web/out/lab/build/*.js` hits jlab_core.46b51ee.js and three other chunks (plus `Function("return this")` in five more), which require 'unsafe-eval', not 'wasm-unsafe-eval'. The header comment's caution is better founded than the finding allows.

**Impact.** Any attempt to add script-src fails CI and must be argued down before it can be shipped. The test's stated justification is also over-broad: `script-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline' 'wasm-unsafe-eval' blob:` satisfies both constraints the comment names (Pyodide's wasm eval, Next.js inline scripts) while still blocking third-party script injection — the exact class the header exists for. Neither #49 nor this is among the filed issues #209-#230.

**Evidence.** ```js
it("stages no full CSP yet (script-src/style-src would break Pyodide + Next inline scripts)", () => {
  const csp = byPattern.get("/**")!.headers["Content-Security-Policy"];
  expect(csp).not.toContain("script-src");
  expect(csp).not.toContain("style-src");
  expect(csp).not.toContain("default-src");
});
```

**Verifier (reproduces) — Partly true, low, ran locally.** The assertion is exactly where the finder says: `grep -n "stages no full CSP" -A 8 web/__tests__/infra/custom-http.test.ts` returns lines 179-183 with the three `expect(csp).not.toContain(...)` calls, and `cd web && npx jest __tests__/infra/custom-http.test.ts` passes 15/15 against `customHttp.yml:43`, so adding `script-src` would indeed turn this suite red. But this is how the whole file is built — the `it.each` blocks at lines 74-105 pin every `Cache-Control` value verbatim too, so any header edit updates its guard in the same commit; `ls .github/` shows only `workflows/` and no CODEOWNERS, so there is no review gate to "argue down." The finder's proposed policy is also asserted rather than tested: with `'unsafe-inline'` present it does not block the inline-injection class it names, and the only `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` in the app (`web/src/app/layout.tsx:94`) is a static locale-bootstrap literal with no user data, so on a static export with no server the reachable XSS surface I could find is nil.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Partly true, low, read.** Both literal facts check out: web/__tests__/infra/custom-http.test.ts:179-183 carries the three negative assertions verbatim, and customHttp.yml:43 ships a CSP with no script-src, and `gh issue list --state all --limit 40` confirms nothing in 209-230 covers it. But `git log -S"stages no full CSP yet" -- web/__tests__/infra/custom-http.test.ts` returns exactly one commit — 1612ceb, the same commit that added the header — so the test is a contemporaneous note on a deliberate deferral written in the same idiom as the rest of that file (the "no unjustified immutable" and "keys disjoint" guards), not a lock retro-fitted over a known gap. Hardening the CSP means editing three lines in the same diff, not winning an argument, and nothing false is shipping to a user meanwhile, so this is a low-severity code-health note whose security substance is entirely the prior audit's #49.

*Raised as medium, published at low — the verification pass set this.*

#### `f150cdd31` lambda/sync persists an unverified `email` claim, and lambda/review-email mails it — Cognito's email attribute is Mutable and SRP sessions can change it

`lambda/sync/index.mjs:48-52`

**low** · Partly true · security · observed live · fix plan 09

**Claim.** The comment calls it "The verified email claim" but the code reads `claims.email` and never looks at `claims.email_verified`. `infra/workspace/cognito.yaml:213-217` declares the `email` attribute `Mutable: true`, and web/src/lib/auth-session.ts:1-8 documents that Cognito grants `aws.cognito.signin.user.admin` on the non-OAuth (SRP) flows — so a native learner can call `UpdateUserAttributes` with any address, refresh, and have that address land on their progress row. lambda/review-email/review-email-core.mjs:257 reads it back (`const email = it.email?.S`) and passes it straight to SES at :275 (`Destination: { ToAddresses: [email] }`).

> **Correction.** The send path is not open. The sender aborts the whole run while SES is in the sandbox (review-email-core.mjs:212-219) and live us-east-2 SES has `ProductionAccessEnabled: false` (production-access review DENIED), so nothing is mailed to anyone today — this is a latent defect that arms on SES approval, not an active unsolicited-mail path. Conversely, the previous audit's dismissal ("self-signup confirms the address") was the wrong refutation: the pool's empty `AttributesRequireVerificationBeforeUpdate` means post-signup email changes take effect immediately, so a confirmed signup proves nothing about the address later stored.

> **Correction.** The mail half of the impact is not live. SES has no production access in either the sender's region or us-east-1 (`ProductionAccessEnabled: false`), so a send to an address the account never verified is rejected, and the sender's daily EventBridge rule is DISABLED. The path is also strictly opt-in rather than a relay: review-email-core.mjs:261-262 skips any user without `remindersOn === true`, CADENCE_DAYS=7 caps it at one fixed-content message per week, and every message carries an unsubscribe link. What stands unconditionally is the smaller half — a deployed handler persisting an attacker-chosen third-party address into quantum-workspace-progress under a comment that claims it is verified, which becomes a live mail path the moment SES leaves sandbox and the rule is enabled. The prior-audit citation is also off: this was identity/security item #8, not "Money path #18".

**Impact.** The platform will send mail from its verified SES domain to an address it never verified, chosen by the account holder. That is an unsolicited-mail path attached to the sender reputation of the production domain, and it stores a third party's address in the progress table with no consent record. The fix already exists in this repo — lambda/qpu/qpu-core.mjs:763 does `claims.email_verified === "true" || claims.email_verified === true` on the spend gate. Prior audit (Money path #18) raised this; it is NOT among the filed issues #209-#230 and is unchanged today.

**Evidence.** ```js
// The verified email claim (ID tokens carry it) — persisted so the review-
// email sender can reach the learner without a Cognito ListUsers lookup.
const email = typeof claims.email === "string" ? claims.email : undefined;
```
and index.test.mjs:68-75 asserts only that a bare `email` claim is stored, so no test can see the missing check.

**Verifier (reproduces) — Partly true, low, observed live.** The mechanism is real and live config makes it stronger than the finder showed: `aws cognito-idp describe-user-pool --user-pool-id $USER_POOL_ID --region us-east-2` returns `UserAttributeUpdateSettings.AttributesRequireVerificationBeforeUpdate: []` with email `Mutable: true` and `WriteAttributes: null` on the client, so an SRP session's `UpdateUserAttributes` swaps the email in place immediately (setting `email_verified=false`), and lambda/sync/index.mjs:52,98 persists that claim with no check while calling it "the verified email claim" (the sync API's authorizer sets `audience: <clientId>`, so it is an ID token and `email_verified` is sitting right there unused). The impact half does not hold today: review-email-core.mjs:212-219 hard-refuses the entire run unless `ProductionAccessEnabled === true`, and `aws sesv2 get-account --region us-east-2` returns `ProductionAccessEnabled: false` with `ReviewDetails.Status: DENIED` — so zero mail leaves that sender, to any address, and the SES-reputation harm is unreachable rather than active. What remains is an unverified third-party address stored on the progress row, a comment that lies about it, and a trap that arms itself the day SES production access is granted; `gh issue list --state all --search email` shows no filed issue covering it, and docs/codebase-audit-2026-08-16.md #8 recorded it as "Partly true" on the weaker signup-only reasoning.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Partly true, low, observed live.** The code facts hold: lambda/sync/index.mjs:48-52 comments "The verified email claim" but reads only `claims.email`, writes it at :98, and lambda/review-email/review-email-core.mjs:252/275 passes it to SES `ToAddresses`, while lambda/qpu/qpu-core.mjs:763 does gate on `email_verified`. The finder's mechanism also survives the check that killed this finding in the prior audit (docs/codebase-audit-2026-08-16.md item #8, whose verifier argued signup-time confirmation makes it unreachable): `aws cognito-idp describe-user-pool --user-pool-id $USER_POOL_ID --region us-east-2` returns `UserAttributeUpdateSettings.AttributesRequireVerificationBeforeUpdate: []`, so an `UpdateUserAttributes` email change takes effect on the token immediately with `email_verified=false`, and `describe-user-pool-client` shows `ALLOW_USER_SRP_AUTH` with default (unrestricted) WriteAttributes, so a native learner can indeed land a stranger's address on their progress row. What does not hold is the impact as written: `aws sesv2 get-account` returns `ProductionAccessEnabled: false` in both us-east-2 (the sender's region) and us-east-1, so SES rejects any unverified recipient today, and `aws events list-rules --region us-east-2` shows `quantum-review-email-SenderFunctionDaily-...` in state `DISABLED`, so the sender never fires.

*Raised as medium, published at low — the verification pass set this.*

#### `f428e799a` The Founding Ten registry ships a real person's full name beside an unsalted SHA-256 of their email, in a public repo and in the public JS bundle

`web/src/data/founding-ten.json:5-7`

**low** · Partly true · security · ran locally · **split verdict** · fix plan 09

**Claim.** scripts/lib/email-hash.mjs:15 justifies the design with "Hashing also means no plaintext address is ever committed." Literally true, but the privacy property it implies does not hold: `emailHash` is `sha256(email.trim().toLowerCase())` with no salt and no key stretching (email-hash.mjs:17-20), and email addresses are low-entropy. An unsalted digest of a low-entropy identifier is a confirmation oracle — anyone can test a candidate address against the committed hash in microseconds. Here the holder's real full name is published on the adjacent line, which reduces the candidate space to a handful of common patterns.

> **Correction.** Two parts of the reasoning are wrong. First, this is not an unexamined design justified by a misleading comment: web/src/lib/founding-ten.ts:16-18 explicitly disclaims the privacy property and names the confirmation-oracle attack ("emails are enumerable, so a guess can be tested against this file"), recording the holder's name as public by consent. Second, the implied fix (salt + key stretching) cannot work here. my-badges.tsx:11-12 compares useAuth().emailHash against the bundled registry in the browser, so any salt or KDF must also ship to the client and is replicable by the attacker. With client-side matching, a confirmation oracle is unavoidable; the only real mitigation is moving badge matching server-side, which is a materially larger change than adding a salt. Also, scripts/founding-credit/cohort-2026-08.json carries slot numbers and timestamps but no names, so for those seven hashes an attacker learns nothing unless they already guessed the exact address.

**Impact.** The registry is not merely repo-visible; it is bundled into the static export and served to every visitor — the hash appears in web/out/_next/static/chunks/0g7fsw8.azcut.js, because web/src/lib/founding-ten.ts:48-50 hashes the signed-in email in the browser and compares client-side. scripts/founding-credit/cohort-2026-08.json:11-17 commits seven more hashes the same way. Anyone can confirm whether a guessed address belongs to a named Founding Ten holder or a credited cohort member.

**Evidence.** ```json
{
  "serial": 1,
  "holder": "$FOUNDING_HOLDER_NAME",
  "issuedAt": "2026-07-29",
  "emailHash": "$FOUNDING_EMAIL_HASH"
}
```
The same digest appears at scripts/founding-credit/cohort-2026-08.json:14 (slot 4) and in the built bundle.

**Verifier (reproduces) — Partly true, low, ran locally.** Every factual claim reproduces: web/src/data/founding-ten.json:5-7 holds "$FOUNDING_HOLDER_NAME" beside an unsalted SHA-256; scripts/lib/email-hash.mjs:17-20 is bare sha256(trim+lowercase) with no salt or stretching; and `grep -rl "$FOUNDING_EMAIL_HASH" web/out/` returns exactly web/out/_next/static/chunks/0g7fsw8.azcut.js, so the digest is served to every visitor. The finder's framing is what fails. It cites the helper comment in email-hash.mjs:15 as the design's justification, but web/src/lib/founding-ten.ts:16-18 — three lines above the hashEmail it cites for impact — already states verbatim: "The hash is not a privacy guarantee (emails are enumerable, so a guess can be tested against this file); it exists so the PUBLIC repo holds no address. The holder's name is already public on their proof page by consent." The exact attack was named and accepted by the author, and roster.tsx:37 plus badge-proof.tsx:29 render the holder's name publicly by design, so the marginal disclosure is only linking an address the attacker already possesses to an already-public name. Keeping severity at low.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Refuted, low, ran locally.** This is documented, deliberate, reasoned-through behaviour, not an oversight. `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-07-29-founding-ten-badges-design.md:54-60` carries a section headed "Accepted weakness, stated plainly" that states the finder's claim almost verbatim — "An email hash is not strong protection — emails are enumerable, so anyone can hash a guess and test it against the registry. Since the holder is already named publicly on the proof page, the marginal disclosure is 'this named person uses this address.' Accepted knowingly, in exchange for a binding that does not silently detach." `web/src/lib/founding-ten.ts:14-16` repeats the disclaimer in code ("The hash is not a privacy guarantee (emails are enumerable, so a guess can be tested against this file); it exists so the PUBLIC repo holds no address"), so the finder's central move — that `email-hash.mjs:15` implies a privacy property that does not hold — is wrong: that comment claims only that no plaintext is committed, which the finder concedes is literally true, and the two authoritative places disclaim the stronger property explicitly. The implied remedy is also inert: `founding-ten.ts:44-52` hashes and compares in the browser against the shipped registry, so any salt would have to ship with it. I ran `grep -rl '$FOUNDING_EMAIL_HASH' web/out` (1 hit) and `grep -rl 'c4bbbc098bd9eb5dac4694a7f5149ed2' web/out` (0 hits): the charter hash does reach the bundle as claimed, but the seven `scripts/founding-credit/cohort-2026-08.json` hashes do not, and they carry no names — so the "name narrows the candidate space" argument does not extend to them.

*Split verdict: reproduces said Partly true; correct as written said Refuted. This one needs a human.*

## 5. The web app and the learner runtime

7 findings — 2 medium, 5 low. 80 files read.

### How it actually works

The web app is a Next 16 / React 19 App Router site compiled with output: "export" (web/next.config.ts) and deployed to Amplify. Twenty-six route files produce the static export; there are three dynamic segments with generateStaticParams (learn/[section], glossary/[term], founding-ten/[badge]), and only the latter two also set dynamicParams = false. Almost all interactivity is client-side. src/app/layout.tsx stacks ThemeProvider, LocaleProvider, AuthProvider, then FogField / Nav / AuthWall / Footer / AskTutor / ProgressSync, and emits a dependency-free pre-hydration script that stamps html lang from the literal "qc:locale" key before any module graph exists.

Persistence is entirely localStorage, namespaced per account by src/lib/progress-owner.ts: the anonymous bucket IS the flat "qc:" namespace and a signed-in account gets "qc:o:<sub>:", so there is no migration to get wrong and no cross-account leak. Every reader (review-store, circuit-store, activity-log, skill-measure) walks ownedLocalKeys(). All of it is surfaced through useSyncExternalStore over one custom "qc-progress" event plus a "qc:"-filtered storage listener (progress-store.ts:74-90), with string/number snapshots chosen for value equality — the reason the static shell hydrates without a mismatch.

The spaced-repetition kernel (review-schedule.ts) is pure SM-2/FSRS arithmetic over injected epoch-days, with overdue credit, a monotonicity clamp, and parseCardState as the one trust boundary. review-store.ts is the only clock/storage edge. /review re-mounts the six graded Rep widgets live via LIVE_WIDGETS, resolved through Object.hasOwn so a corrupt stored kind cannot reach Object through the prototype chain.

Heavy runtimes are same-origin and version-pinned: Monaco is staged under /monaco/<version>/vs (monaco-path.ts), and Pyodide runs in a dedicated worker with boot/run watchdogs and a CDN fallback carrying a hand-verified SRI digest (pyodide-runtime.ts). Lesson widgets are next/dynamic(ssr:false) behind an IntersectionObserver approach gate and one shared WidgetErrorBoundary (widget-fence.tsx:137).

The seams are at the edges. Lessons are rendered client-side: lesson-body.tsx is "use client" and receives both markdownEn and markdownEs, so react-markdown + katex + highlight and ~50KB of raw GUIDE prose per locale ship to the browser. Both i18n dictionaries (95KB of source) are statically imported by translate.ts, which the root LocaleProvider pulls into the shared chunk on every page including the public funnel. And the money surfaces — qpu-submit-panel.tsx, playground/hardware-panel.tsx, the whole of /workspace — sit outside i18n and outside pricing.ts, deriving their own credit figures from the raw provider table in components/quantum/cost.ts.

### Done well

- progress-owner.ts:98-120 makes the anonymous bucket literally identical to the legacy flat namespace ('qc:card:x') while accounts get 'qc:o:<sub>:card:x'. That asymmetry means pre-existing unowned data needs no migration, no rename, and no window where history looks deleted — and accounts still cannot see each other. ownedLocalKeys() then explicitly excludes SCOPE_PREFIX from the anon scan (progress-owner.ts:136-138), which is the one line that stops 'anonymous' from seeing everyone.
- review-dashboard.tsx:106 resolves a stored card kind with `kind && Object.hasOwn(LIVE_WIDGETS, kind)`. The comment names the exact failure it prevents: LIVE_WIDGETS["constructor"] resolves through the prototype chain to Object, which React would then invoke and throw on, taking out the whole /review route. review-store.ts:196 uses the identical guard for kindBucket. This is a real prototype-pollution-adjacent bug closed in two places.
- code-editor.tsx:74-86 solves WCAG 2.1.2 for Monaco properly: Escape (conditioned on `!suggestWidgetVisible && !findWidgetVisible`) arms tabFocusMode so the next Tab leaves the editor, onDidFocusEditorText restores Tab-as-indent, and code-editor.tsx:126-129 renders a permanently visible, screen-reader-reachable sentence disclosing the escape. Most editor integrations ship the keyboard trap and no disclosure.
- monaco-path.ts:28-31 stamps MONACO_VERSION into the asset path and scripts/stage-monaco.mjs fails the build if the constant disagrees with the installed package. The header spells out the failure it prevents: /monaco/** is served immutable for a year while Monaco's own entry filenames are unhashed, so a returning learner would request hashed chunks a new deploy no longer serves and could not recover without a hard reload.
- ask-tutor.tsx:31-45 uses an idle watchdog re-armed on every stream chunk rather than a total-duration cap, and states why: a duration cap armed once cannot distinguish a stalled connection from a long healthy answer. ask-tutor.tsx:196 keys the abort cleanup on `slug` rather than an empty dep array, because the component lives in the root layout and an empty-dep cleanup would only fire when the layout unmounts — never on a route change.
- fog-field.tsx:243-250 subscribes to a live `prefers-reduced-motion` MediaQueryList change event so a mid-session OS toggle freezes or resumes the canvas immediately, and the cleanup at 251-258 cancels the rAF, the resize debounce, the resize listener, the media listener and the MutationObserver. Every observer and listener in web/src is balanced; the sweep for addEventListener without a matching removeEventListener returned nothing.
- pricing-page-content.tsx:161-180 places the not-yet-metered disclosure ABOVE the tier grid and the top-up, and the comment records that it previously sat below all three purchase controls. __tests__/app/pricing-page.test.tsx asserts document position against every purchase control, so the placement cannot silently regress — a structural guard rather than a string match.

### Findings

#### `f0f7d9816` Four status live regions are mounted at the same moment their content appears, so screen readers may never announce them

`web/src/components/quantum/review-card.tsx:211-213`

**medium** · Confirmed · accessibility · ran locally · fix plan 11

**Claim.** role="status" nodes are rendered conditionally rather than kept in the DOM and filled. An aria-live region that is inserted together with its text is unreliably announced (notably VoiceOver/Safari and NVDA), because there is no mutation to an existing live region for the AT to observe.

> **Correction.** Two details in the write-up are off. The counter-example lives at web/src/components/ask-tutor.tsx:476, not quantum/ask-tutor.tsx:452-454 — and stronger in-repo precedents exist (challenge.tsx:256-258, debug-circuit-widget.tsx:216-219 spell out the reasoning explicitly). Also, "a screen-reader user gets silence" is exact for the review card, quiz and session-complete banner, but overstated for the auth resend: `setResendCooldown(30)` (auth-form.tsx:200) relabels and disables the just-clicked, still-focused button, so most screen readers announce something — a countdown, not a confirmation the code was sent. That makes auth-form the weakest of the four, not an equal peer.

> **Correction.** Two small cite corrections that do not affect the verdict. The exemplar persistent region in ask-tutor.tsx is at lines 474-478, not 452-454 (line 452 is inside the "Deliberately NOT a live region" comment on the streaming answer). And "a screen-reader user gets silence" is stronger than the mechanism warrants: the failure is AT/browser-dependent (reliably bad on VoiceOver/Safari, version-dependent on NVDA), and the text persists on screen and in the DOM afterward, so what is lost is the automatic announcement, not access to the information.

**Impact.** The four affected regions carry exactly the confirmations a non-sighted learner has no other way to perceive: the review card's and the quiz's post-grade schedule note ('next review in N days' / 'noop'), the /review session-complete banner, and the auth form's 'your code is on the way' after a resend. A sighted user sees a fade-up; a screen-reader user gets silence and cannot tell whether the grade registered or the code was resent. The codebase already knows the correct pattern — ask-tutor.tsx:452-454 keeps `<p role="status" className="sr-only">{srStatus}</p>` permanently mounted and writes into it — so this is drift, not ignorance.

**Evidence.** review-card.tsx:211-213 `{outcome && (\n  <p\n    role="status"` — the region does not exist until `outcome` is set. Identically: quiz.tsx:294-296 `{outcome && (<p role="status"`, review-dashboard.tsx:189-191 `{sessionComplete && (<div role="status"`, auth-form.tsx:346-347 `{resendState === "sent" && (<p role="status"`.

**Verifier (reproduces) — Confirmed, medium, ran locally.** All four sites reproduce verbatim: review-card.tsx:211-213, quiz.tsx:294-296, review-dashboard.tsx:189-191 and auth-form.tsx:346-347 each wrap the live region in the same condition that supplies its text, so the node does not exist until the moment it is filled. I looked for an escape hatch and found none — `grep -n "useRef\|focus(\|LiveStatus" review-card.tsx review-dashboard.tsx` returns nothing, so there is no focus move and no sibling always-mounted region, and these are shipped free-forever surfaces (R1), not dormant money paths. The codebase states the exact rationale against this pattern in its own comments at challenge.tsx:256-258 and debug-circuit-widget.tsx:216-218 ("mounting a fresh region per verdict is not reliably announced"), and all six ScheduleNote call sites comply, so this is genuine drift from an enforced internal standard. `npx jest __tests__/components/quantum/review-card.test.tsx` passes 9/9 while asserting nothing about the region, confirming no guard exists. Held at medium rather than high because the text stays reachable by browsing and the failure is AT/browser-dependent rather than guaranteed.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Confirmed, medium, read.** All four cited nodes are genuinely conditional mounts, not text swaps: `web/src/components/quantum/review-card.tsx:211-213` (`{outcome && (<p role="status"`), `web/src/components/quantum/quiz.tsx:294-296`, `web/src/components/review-dashboard.tsx:189-191` (`{sessionComplete && (<div role="status"`), and `web/src/components/auth/auth-form.tsx:346-347` — React renders `null` until the flag flips, so the live region does not exist in the accessibility tree before its content arrives. The finder's rule is not an outside opinion imported into this codebase; it is the codebase's own written convention, stated verbatim at `web/src/components/quantum/predict-widget.tsx:74-76` ("mounting a new role=status with its text announces inconsistently"), `web/src/components/live-status.tsx:7` ("the node stays mounted so aria-live fires on text change"), `web/src/components/workspace/masthead.tsx:49-51`, and `web/src/components/quantum/widget-ui.tsx:332-334` — five sibling widgets keep a persistent region plus a focus target, and these four do not. I checked for compensating mechanisms and found none: `grep -n 'useRef\|\.focus()'` over review-card.tsx, quiz.tsx and review-dashboard.tsx returns nothing, so there is no focus move to carry the announcement, and in review-card `onGrade` also calls `setRevealed(false)`, which unmounts the very rating button that held focus. Not high: the outcome text stays in the DOM and remains reachable by browse-mode navigation, and metering/storefront rules are untouched here — this degrades confirmation, not information.

#### `fe95b2089` The pricing page and the QPU run surface publish two different credit prices for the same IQM Garnet run

`web/src/components/quantum/qpu-submit-panel.tsx:332`

**medium** · Partly true · correctness · observed live · fix plan 04

**Claim.** /pricing derives hardware cost from the published sheet in lib/pricing.ts (TASK_FEE_CREDITS = 34, IQM Garnet creditsPerShot = 0.163), while the QPU wallet panel and confirm step derive it independently from the raw AWS table in components/quantum/cost.ts (PRICING.IQM = {perTask: 0.3, perShot: 0.00145}). The two disagree on every run size.

> **Correction.** Two parts of the framing overstate it. (1) The rule 8 / rule 13 charge does not hold: rule 8 forbids RESTATING the published figures elsewhere, and the panel does not restate them — it derives a different quantity (raw AWS cost, from cost.ts, parity-locked to lib/utils/cost.py) — while /pricing already says "hardware runs are not currently available" and labels the sheet a forecast of launch pricing, so nothing is being advertised that the system cannot do. (2) The claim that "what is not recorded is that it already understates against the published sheet today" is not new information: CLAUDE.md's open-work item already records that the published per-task and per-shot credit figures do not stand in one common ratio to the provider's two list rates (34 credits vs $0.30 = 1.133x; 0.163 vs $0.00145 = 1.124x), which is exactly the statement that the sheet sits above raw cost, and en.ts:842-846 records the two-table split in a comment. The genuine defect left over is narrow and cosmetic-until-cutover: two user-visible credit figures for the same run on two pages a "Top up" link connects.

> **Correction.** Three parts of the finder's framing are wrong. (1) Rule 8 is misattributed: it governs pricing.ts ↔ CATALOG lockstep and bars restating the published numbers; components/quantum/cost.ts restates nothing published — it is the AWS provider-cost table mirrored from lib/utils/cost.py, which CLAUDE.md explicitly separates from customer prices ("These are our AWS costs... not customer-facing prices"). The cleaner citation is rule 9 (QPU debits at the same markup as the tutor) plus the honesty concern, not rule 8. (2) "It becomes a wrong debit the moment either is switched on" is false: lambda/qpu/qpu-core.mjs:97-99 and :431 compute the debit as ceil(cost * factor / MICROS_PER_CREDIT) with a REQUIRED injected factor, so the panel's number is a display quote only — the failure mode is an understated quote, exactly what CLAUDE.md already records ("the confirm screen would understate the debit"). (3) The divergence is not undocumented: cost-estimator.tsx:80-90 records it with the same $SPREAD_PCT delta and pricing-page.test.tsx:404-414 bars copy that calls the two one estimate. What is genuinely new is narrower and worth stating that way: that comment's reconciliation ("different table, different currency") went stale when the panel started quoting CREDITS in #189/#191 (2026-07-27/28), after the comment landed (#188, 2026-07-26) — so the surfaces now publish two credit figures, not a credit figure and a dollar figure.

**Impact.** A learner who reads /pricing sees a 1,000-shot IQM Garnet run cost 197 credits ($1.97); the panel that actually spends the credits tells them 175. At 100 shots it is 50.3 versus 45. The panel's sentence 'Runs are billed at the Amazon Braket price with no markup' is the load-bearing trust claim on the spend surface, and it is contradicted by the site's own published sheet — which __tests__/lib/pricing.test.ts:59 explicitly permits to sit above provider cost. This violates rule 8 (published rates are defined in pricing.ts and must not be restated elsewhere) and rule 13. No money moves today (metering off, storefront closed), which is why this is medium and not high; it becomes a wrong debit the moment either is switched on. Note CLAUDE.md already records that the confirm step 'would understate the debit' UNDER a deployed factor — what is not recorded is that it already understates against the published sheet today, with no factor deployed.

**Evidence.** qpu-submit-panel.tsx:332 `const runCost = creditsForMicros(costMicros(100));` renders at :349 as `costs {runCost} credits.` -> ceil((300000 + 145000)/10000) = 45. cost-estimator.tsx:103 `const runCredits = jobCredits(device, shots);` and pricing-page-content.tsx's HARDWARE_RATES table use pricing.ts:147 `rate.creditsPerShot * shots + (rate.perTask ? TASK_FEE_CREDITS : 0)` -> 0.163*100 + 34 = 50.3. Sources: pricing.ts:60 `export const TASK_FEE_CREDITS = 34;`, pricing.ts:85 `creditsPerShot: 0.163,`; qpu-budget.ts:23-25 `IQM_TASK_MICROS = Math.round(PRICING.IQM.perTask * 1_000_000)` over cost.ts:6 `IQM: { perTask: 0.3, perShot: 0.00145 }`.

**Verifier (reproduces) — Partly true, low, observed live.** The arithmetic reproduces: qpu-submit-panel.tsx:332 `creditsForMicros(costMicros(100))` over qpu-budget.ts:23-25 / cost.ts:6 yields 45 credits (175 at 1,000 shots), while pricing.ts:147 `jobCredits` over TASK_FEE_CREDITS=34 + creditsPerShot=0.163 yields 50.3 (197) — I ran `node -e` on both formulas to confirm 45/50.3 and 175/197. But the divergence is deliberate and disclosed, not an undetected bug: `web/__tests__/lib/pricing.test.ts:44-64` exists specifically to assert the published sheet sits ABOVE provider list rate (that gap is the private markup, rule 6), and the shipped pricing-page copy in BOTH locales (`pricingUi.priceHardwareBody`, en.ts:849 / es.ts:787, rendered at cost-estimator.tsx:118) tells the learner the workspace pre-flight quotes "at the AWS list rate — not the credit figure shown here", with a code comment at en.ts:842-846 stating the pre-flight uses a different table on purpose. Nothing debits either way: `aws lambda get-function-configuration --function-name quantum-qpu-submit --query 'Environment.Variables'` returns only LEDGER_TABLE/EDGE_SECRET/TASKS_TABLE/RESULTS_BUCKET — no wallet table, so no credits move; `aws amplify get-app --app-id d1ao02to23x85y` does show NEXT_PUBLIC_QPU_URL set, so the 45-credit card is visible on /workspace today, which is the only real residue: a signed-in learner reads 45 in the workspace and 50.3 on /pricing.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Partly true, medium, observed live.** The two figures are real and both are shipped. Source: pricing-page-content.tsx:394-395 + pricing.ts:60,85 give jobCredits(IQM Garnet, 1000) = 197 credits, while qpu-budget.ts:23-25,34 over cost.ts:6 gives creditsForMicros(costMicros(1000)) = 175, rendered by qpu-submit-panel.tsx:332/349 (WalletBar) and :1245 (wallet-funded confirm) — I recomputed both with `node -e` and got 45/175 vs 50.3/197. Live check: `curl -s https://quantum.altivum.ai/pricing | grep -o "197\|0.163"` returns both published values, and `curl -s https://quantum.altivum.ai/_next/static/chunks/0f6lbc6u4b8ea.js` contains the panel copy "That exact figure is what comes out of your wallet — the credit cost below is the AWS price converted at $0.01 per credit" with `qpuUrl()` inlined as a real `https://$API_HOST`, so the panel is configured and live rather than inert. So a signed-in learner can see two different credit prices for the same IQM Garnet run today, and the panel's "no markup / at cost" promise contradicts a sheet whose own test (__tests__/lib/pricing.test.ts:56-60) asserts published rates sit at or above provider list cost.

#### `f3f8d7a2e` /workspace is the only walled route with no metadata at all, and auth-wall.tsx documents a noindex protection it does not have

`web/src/app/workspace/page.tsx:1`

**low** · Confirmed · consistency · ran locally · fix plan 11

**Claim.** workspace/page.tsx opens with "use client", which makes a metadata export illegal, so the route ships with no title, no description and — unlike every other gated route — no robots directive. AuthWall's own comment claims the opposite as the security argument for gating client-side.

**Impact.** The exported /workspace/index.html inherits only the layout title, so the browser tab and any share card read as the generic site name; and it is the one walled URL a crawler is free to index. The consequence is limited (the page's rendered DOM is the auth gate, and it is absent from sitemap.ts's allowlist), but the comment at auth-wall.tsx:50 is the recorded justification for treating a client-side gate as sufficient, and it is not true for this route. /login has the same shape but is deliberately public, so /workspace is the sole real gap. Prior audit findings #33/#43; confirmed unchanged.

**Evidence.** web/src/app/workspace/page.tsx:1 `"use client";` — the file has no `export const metadata`. Compare review/page.tsx:6 `robots: { index: false, follow: false },`, runbook/page.tsx, credentials/page.tsx, playground/page.tsx and learn/[section]/page.tsx:39, all of which set it. auth-wall.tsx:49-51 `the rendered DOM shows only the gate, and the per-page robots noindex keeps the walled URLs out of search indexes.`

**Verifier (reproduces) — Confirmed, low, ran locally.** `cat web/src/app/workspace/page.tsx` confirms line 1 is `"use client";` with no `export const metadata`, and `ls web/src/app/workspace/` shows page.tsx is the only file (no route layout.tsx exists anywhere except the root), so no route-level metadata is possible as written. Running `grep -o '<title>[^<]*</title>' && grep -o '<meta name="robots" content="[^"]*"'` over the existing static export in `web/out` gives `workspace.html` → `<title>Quantum Learner</title>` + NO ROBOTS META, versus `review/runbook/credentials/playground.html` → `Review — Quantum Learner` + `noindex, nofollow`; `grep -rn "index: false" web/src/app` lists 10 routes and `/workspace` is the only walled one absent (login/auth-callback are in AuthWall's PUBLIC_PATHS). The comment at web/src/components/auth/auth-wall.tsx:49-51 ("the per-page robots noindex keeps the walled URLs out of search indexes") is therefore false for this route, and web/src/app/sitemap.ts:9-12 repeats the same false claim by name ("workspace … carry a noindex meta") — an extra instance the finder did not cite. Real exposure is small: robots.ts allows `/` but no public page emits an `href="/workspace"` (grep over web/out/index.html, pricing.html, privacy.html finds no such href) and web/__tests__/app/sitemap.test.ts:26 pins its sitemap exclusion, so this is an unguarded consistency/documentation gap rather than a live leak.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Confirmed, low, read.** web/src/app/workspace/page.tsx:1 is `"use client";` with no `export const metadata`, and there is no workspace/layout.tsx — the only layout is web/src/app/layout.tsx:42, which sets title/description but no robots. Surveying every page.tsx confirms the finder's exclusivity claim: /credentials, /glossary, /glossary/[term], /learn/[section] (:39), /playground, /review (:7, not :6), /runbook all set `robots: { index: false, follow: false }`; the only other "use client" pages are /login and /auth/callback, both listed in auth-wall.tsx's PUBLIC_PATHS, so /workspace is the sole walled route without a robots directive. The stale local build in web/out corroborates it — `grep -o '<title>[^<]*</title>' web/out/workspace.html` returns the bare layout title `Quantum Learner` and `grep '<meta name="robots"' web/out/workspace.html` returns nothing, while review.html/runbook.html/credentials.html/playground.html each carry `noindex, nofollow` and a page-specific title. The comment at auth-wall.tsx:50 ("the per-page robots noindex keeps the walled URLs out of search indexes") is therefore false for this one route; the finder missed a second copy of the same false claim at web/src/app/sitemap.ts:11 ("those routes carry a noindex meta"). Severity stays low: web/out/workspace.html's prerendered body is only the gate ("Checking your access…") plus nav/footer, so nothing is exposed — the cost is a generically-titled thin page that a crawler may index.

#### `f0d2975d1` consumeHandoff() runs inside a render-phase useState initializer, so a discarded render silently destroys the playground-to-workspace circuit

`web/src/components/quantum/qpu-submit-panel.tsx:902`

**low** · Partly true · correctness · ran locally · **split verdict** · fix plan 11

**Claim.** SubmitForm's first hook performs a destructive sessionStorage mutation during render. consumeHandoff() reads the one-shot handoff and immediately removeItem()s it, so the read cannot be replayed.

> **Correction.** One sub-claim does survive, at much lower stakes: a genuine unmount/remount of SubmitForm does lose the handoff (my harness's step B remounts and renders `PRESET`). But that can only be reached after a submit has already succeeded and the follow-up `refresh()` fails, and such a remount destroys every other form field too (qasm edits, shots, phase, idempotency key) — the handoff is not distinctively at risk. The initializer is still impure and would be better as a `useEffect`/`useRef` consume, but that is a hygiene point, not the described silent data loss.

> **Correction.** StrictMode does not destroy the handoff, so the dev-mode trigger should be dropped. React keeps the first initializer call's value and discards the second (react-dom-client.development.js:8264-8277), and the StrictMode re-render pass never invokes the initializer at all. The unmount/remount trigger is also narrower than stated: `refresh` reaches SubmitForm only as `onSubmitted` (:998, :1048, :1065 — all post-submit) and as CredentialGate's `onEarned` (:821, before SubmitForm exists), so a throttled/errored refresh tears the form down only after the run was already submitted with the handoff circuit. That teardown does wipe the editor contents unrecoverably — a real state-loss bug of the coarse `load === "ready"` gating at :236 that the destructive read makes permanent — but it is not the handoff being lost before first use. What stands unqualified is the interrupted-concurrent-render case and the impurity itself.

**Impact.** React may render and discard a component (concurrent rendering, an interrupted or replayed mount, StrictMode's double render in dev). If the render that consumed the handoff is thrown away, the key is already gone and the remount falls back to `PRESETS[0].qasm` — the learner's circuit from /playground is silently replaced by a preset with no error and nothing logged. The same holds for any unmount/remount of SubmitForm, which is conditionally rendered behind `load === "ready" && budget && challenge && budget.credentialed` (qpu-submit-panel.tsx:236, 252-258), so a refresh() that transiently returns throttled or error tears it down. The inline comment defends the wrong invariant: state initializers not re-firing on RE-RENDER says nothing about a discarded or repeated MOUNT.

**Evidence.** qpu-submit-panel.tsx:902 `const [handoff] = useState(() => consumeHandoff());` with the comment at :901 `State initializers run exactly once, so the consume never re-fires on re-render.` qpu-handoff.ts:61-68 `export function consumeHandoff(): Handoff | null { const h = readValid(); try { sessionStorage.removeItem(KEY); } ... }`. The fallback is qpu-submit-panel.tsx:905 `useState(handoff?.qasm ?? PRESETS[0].qasm)`.

**Verifier (reproduces) — Refuted, low, ran locally.** I reproduced the exact hook pair from qpu-submit-panel.tsx:902-905 against the repo's own React (19.2.4 / react-dom 19.2.4, `node -e "require('react/package.json').version"`) in a jsdom harness: `node /private/tmp/claude-501/-Users-cperez-dev-altivum-inc-quantum/960ed632-66f4-4518-9cd6-a7e8a35074be/scratchpad/strict2.cjs`. Under `<StrictMode>` the initializer is invoked twice (so the removeItem does fire twice) but React discards the second invocation's result — both render passes and the committed DOM show `PLAYGROUND_CIRCUIT`, not the preset — so the headline dev-mode vector does not reproduce. The production "discarded concurrent render" vector is also unreachable here: SubmitForm mounts from `setState` calls inside `refresh()`'s promise continuation (qpu-submit-panel.tsx:170-189), i.e. a DefaultLane update, which React renders as a blocking, non-time-sliced pass with no yield point to be interrupted at, and there is no Suspense/Activity boundary in this subtree. The finder's remaining vector — a `refresh()` that returns throttled/error tearing the form down — is ordered out: `grep -n "refresh" src/components/quantum/qpu-submit-panel.tsx` shows only three call sites (Panel mount, CredentialGate `onEarned` before the form exists, and SubmitForm's own `onSubmitted`), with no polling or focus refetch, so no refresh can unmount the form before the learner has already submitted the circuit.

**Verifier (correct as written) — Partly true, low, ran locally.** The defect is real: qpu-submit-panel.tsx:902 runs `useState(() => consumeHandoff())`, and qpu-handoff.ts:61-68 does readValid() then sessionStorage.removeItem() — an unreplayable destructive side effect in a render-phase initializer, which React's useState contract explicitly requires to be pure, so any render that consumes but never commits drops the learner to PRESETS[0].qasm at :904 with nothing logged. Nothing guards it: the four handoff tests at web/__tests__/components/quantum/qpu-submit-panel.test.tsx:1115-1174 only cover the committed happy path. But the finder's most-cited trigger is wrong — I ran `cd web && node -e "<jsdom + react-dom/client createRoot, React.StrictMode, useState(() => consumeHandoff())>"` against the installed React 19.2.4 and got `consumeHandoff call count: 2 / rendered editor value: HANDOFF_QASM`; react-dom-client.development.js:8264-8277 keeps the FIRST initializer result and discards the StrictMode second call, and the second component pass uses HooksDispatcherOnRerenderInDEV whose useState is rerenderReducer(basicStateReducer) and never calls the initializer. Severity low: this is the free playground-to-workspace path, not a money path, the surviving trigger needs a higher-priority update to interrupt the exact default-lane render that mounts the form, and the worst outcome is a learner seeing the Bell preset and re-clicking from /playground.

*Split verdict: reproduces said Refuted; correct as written said Partly true. This one needs a human.*

*Raised as medium, published at low — the verification pass set this.*

#### `f10ee9469` pricing.ts documents a tutor architecture that the 2026-08-17 Anthropic migration deleted, and that stale claim is the recorded justification for a rule-13 copy decision

`web/src/lib/pricing.ts:162-168`

**low** · Partly true · docs-drift · observed live · fix plan 08
